UK floats fast-track approvals for 'urgent' oil, gas tiebacks
Regulator NSTA urged to weigh applications
Tiebacks extend existing field lifespans
Follows Jackdaw sign-off, Rosebank still due
The UK government could grant licenses to a small number of "urgent" tieback projects - designed to extend lifespans of existing oil and gas fields - before new tieback legislation is introduced, according to an intergovernmental letter released Oct. 26, in the latest sign of a shifting energy approach from Andy Burnham's government.
In a letter to Stuart Payne, chief executive of the upstream regulator the North Sea Transition Authority, Michael Shanks, the minister of state for energy, reiterated plans to introduce Transitional Energy Certificates allowing companies to go after known hydrocarbon deposits close to existing fields, while maintaining its ban on new exploration.
"These reforms will be brought forward through legislation when parliamentary time allows," Shanks wrote in the letter dated Oct. 21 and posted Oct. 26 on the NSTA's website. "In the meantime, we recognize that a small number of projects may face near-term investment and operational timelines that cannot await the passage of the legislation."
He added: "I am therefore asking the NSTA to consider whether there are any such projects that may warrant an earlier licensing decision so that the NSTA can take action to invite, assess and determine applications for them."
The minister noted that the move should not "be regarded as a new plan or program of work or wider reopening of licenses." Instead it will apply in "urgent cases," he said, adding that the

Singapore to store Saudi crude at Jurong Rock Caverns from H2 2027
Aramco, Singapore authorities exchange MOU
Negotiations to conclude in a few months
Singapore and Saudi Arabia have announced their joint intent to establish an arrangement for Saudi crude oil to be stored at the Jurong Rock Caverns from the second half of 2027, Singapore's Minister for Energy, Trade and Industry Tan See Leng said at the Singapore International Energy Week on Oct. 26.
"This arrangement enhances the resilience of the Saudi crude oil supply chain in Southeast Asia, as well as contributes to Singapore's energy security and resilience in the event of supply disruptions," Tan said.
Singapore's Energy Market Authority and Jurong Town Corp. exchanged a memorandum of understanding with Saudi Aramco at SIEW on the day as a first step toward establishing the arrangement, according to a release from the Ministry of Energy, Trade and Industry. Tan said in his speech that the parties aim to conclude negotiations within a few months.
"Singapore's Ministry of Energy, Trade and Industry and the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia's Ministry of Energy will work closely to advance the arrangement and coordinate its implementation with relevant entities," according to the release.
A METI spokesperson told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, on Oct. 26, "As these arrangements are commercially sensitive, we are unable to disclose further details."
Saudi Aramco declined to comment on Oct. 26. Jurong Rock Caverns, comprising four caverns with 330,000 cubic meters each and one with 150,000 cu m, can store up to 1.47 million cu m, equivalent to about 9.25 million barrels of liquid hydrocarbons such as crude oil and condensate, according to

Asia Pacific
China clean product exports likely to fall in Nov: refiners, analysts
Gasoil exports fall 40% on supply shortage Refinery margins turn negative on freight costs Domestic stocks hit lowest since Jan 2023: JLC
Chinese refineries are likely to slash their clean oil product exports in November, led by gasoil, due to tight domestic supplies, while weak refining margins weigh on production, six refiners and two market analysts told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, on Oct. 26.
One of the refiners said the total volume of oil companies allowed to export would fall by 30%, with gasoil volumes down about 40%. However, he did not provide details on the basis for the reductions.
China was estimated to export about 3.7 million metric tons of oil products, comprising about 40 million mt of gasoline, about 1.1 million mt of gasoil and 2.2 million mt of jet fuel, in October, Platts reported earlier. The country's gasoil exports reached a 43-month high of 1.85 million mt in September, pushing total clean oil product exports up 3.7% month over month to 4.41 million mt, according to customs data.
"Exports in November are set to fall as the domestic market is so tight," said a source with a state-run refinery in southern China, adding that Chinese refineries are required to prioritize meeting domestic supplies rather than capturing export profits.
China's gasoil stock fell about 4% month over month to 10.78 million mt on Oct. 23, the lowest since Jan. 6, 2023, according to data from local information provider JLC.
Meanwhile, five of the six refiners who spoke to Platts said they would cut their utilization in November, capping production, as they expected to make losses due to high freight and insurance costs.
"For us, feedstock is sufficient, but the refining margins in the domestic market will worsen next month as soaring freight costs are largely not reflected in China's gasoline and gasoil pricing mechanism," said a source with another state-run refinery. He

added that the refinery plans to cut utilization to about 80% in November from about 93% in October.
Under China's current oil product pricing mechanism, introduced in 2013, the government adjusts retail ceiling prices for gasoline and gasoil every 10 working days based on a basket of benchmark crude prices, reportedly including ICE Brent, NYMEX WTI and DME Oman, weighted according to China's crude import profile.
Market sources said freight and insurance were set at a combined $5/b in the formula, much lower than the current level.
Dirty freight for a 270,000-mt VLCC carrying crude from the Gulf to China has hovered around $230/mt since Sept. 16, according to Platts. The assessment fell to $234.93/mt on Oct. 23 from a record $255.72/mt on Oct. 12, Platts data showed. A year ago, the assessment was at $18.85/mt on Oct. 23, 2025, according to Platts data.
"Margins are deeply negative. Luckily, we will be undergoing maintenance next month," a second source with a state-owned refinery in East China said.
-- Daisy Xu - Oil Market Specialist, Oceana Zhou - Oil Market Specialist
China PDH operating rates edge up on restarts; tight LPG supply caps recovery
PDH margins turn positive at 201 yuan/mt
1.35 mil mt/year capacity due back from maintenance
Tight LPG imports remain key constraint on run rates
China's propane dehydrogenation plant operating rates edged higher in the second half of October as units restarted from maintenance, but tight imported LPG availability continued to constrain a broader recovery in run rates, market sources said.
PDH plant utilization rose to 66.33% as of Oct. 26, from 65.49% a week earlier, according to data from local energy information provider JLC. The rate, however, remained below the September average of 71.39%, suggesting October utilization was likely to remain below the previous month's level.
"The utilization rate improved with a few plants restarting units," a JLC analyst said.
Operating rates could recover further, with plants totaling 1.35 million mt/year of PDH capacity scheduled to restart following maintenance, potentially providing additional support to China's demand for imported propane.
The returning capacity includes Zhenhua Petrochemical's 750,000 mt/year PDH unit and Jvzhengyuan's 600,000 mt/ year facility, according to the analyst. Guoheng Petrochemical's 660,000 mt/year PDH unit restarted in the week to Oct. 25.
LPG supply remains tight Despite the planned restarts, limited arrivals of imported LPG
could cap plant utilization, market sources said.

"There is not much improvement on the imported feedstock," the JLC analyst said, adding that overall LPG arrivals during Oct. 19-23 were around 30% lower than the previous week.
An East China-based analyst also pointed to lingering supply constraints, saying lower LPG supply from the Middle East could not be fully compensated by cargoes from other regions, including the US.
China's combined LPG imports from the UAE, Oman, Qatar, Kuwait and Saudi Arabia fell 48.8% year over year to 6.86 million mt over January-September, data from the General Administration of Customs showed. The five suppliers accounted for 32.4% of China's LPG imports over the period, down from 49.5% a year earlier, with volumes from each supplier declining.
Meanwhile, US LPG flows to China were tracking at around 1 million mt for October despite the 10% tariff remaining in place. Some cargoes currently indicated for Japan could also be redirected to China, potentially lifting October arrivals above September levels, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.
Improved margins offer support Improving PDH processing economics could provide further
support for operating rates as feedstock becomes available. The margin for converting propane into propylene was at 201
yuan/mt as of Oct. 25, compared with an average loss of 36 yuan/ mt in September, according to JLC data.

Europe, Middle East and Africa
Algeria to send troops to Niger to protect energy facilities
Algeria's first foreign deployment since 1973 Sonatrach is drilling in Niger's Kafra oil field Nations are Trans-Saharan gas pipeline partners
Algeria is set to deploy military units to neighboring Niger to protect energy infrastructure in the unstable Sahel nation, where

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

3

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.



Platts Oilgram News

October 26, 2026

Algeria's national oil company is drilling for crude, according to a statement from the presidency.
In the Oct. 26 release, the office of Abdelmadjid Tebboune said the deployment would "ensure the protection of shared AlgerianNigerien energy and economic interests".
It follows a request from Niger's ruling junta in Niamey and approval from both chambers of Algeria's legislature, the Council of the Nation and the People's National Assembly on Oct. 25, the statement added.
The foreign deployment - Algeria's first since 1973 - comes after Niger's defense minister visited Algiers to seek help in the West African country's fight against terrorist groups, including those linked to al-Qaeda and the Islamic State.
It also comes after Algeria sent fighter jets to Niger in August to help see off an attempted coup against Gen. Abdourahamane Tchiani, who took power in Niger in 2023 in a coup of his own that ousted elected president Mohamed Bazoum.
The Algerian presidency did not provide any details about the likely length or scope of the deployment or the number of troops that could be involved.
State-run Sonatrach - which has been expanding its African footprint in the past year - is currently undertaking hydrocarbon exploration in Niger's Kafra oil field.
In June, Sonatrach and its Nigerien equivalent Sonidep signed three MoUs covering seismic acquisition and processing, oil and gas drilling and refined product distribution. Algeria is also a fuel supplier to landlocked Niger.
The two countries are also partners in the Trans Saharan Gas Pipeline, an ambitious project designed to move gas from Nigeria to Algeria and onto Europe via Niger. The project was thrown into jeopardy after the 2023 Niger coup, which strained relations between Niamey and Abuja.
Nevertheless, Algerian officials remain optimistic, with the country starting construction on its leg of the pipe in June, according to its hydrocarbon ministry.
Niger is reliant on crude exports and imported refined products. It exports around 110,000 barrels/day of its heavy sweet Meleck crude through the Niger-Benin pipeline and onto international markets.
-- Charlie Mitchell
Galp, Moeve call off Iberian downstream merger plan
Negotiations fail on strategic terms
Three refineries remain separate entities
Portugal cited sovereignty over Sines plant
A proposed merger of downstream assets belonging to Portugal's Galp Energia SGPS SA and Spain-based Moeve SA has resulted in no agreement, the two companies said in separate statements Oct. 26.
"Further to the announcement of January 8, 2026, regarding a non-binding agreement between Galp and Moeve's shareholders, Mubadala Investment Company and The Carlyle Group, and

following extensive negotiations between the parties, no agreement has been reached," Galp said.
In a separate statement Oct. 26, Moeve said that it and Galp "did not reach final terms that met their respective strategic and financial criteria to support an enduring and value-accretive partnership."
"Following extensive negotiations and a strong commitment from all parties to explore the potential of this combination, and despite all the efforts, we have been unable to reach an agreement on an opportunity of significant potential to create a major European downstream player," Galp Chairperson Paula Amorim said, adding that the parties had been unable to achieve the necessary commercial alignment.
"We remain fully focused on executing our transformation with financial discipline and delivering sustainable long-term value," Moeve CEO Maarten Wetselaar said.
According to Wetselaar, the potential downstream "combination with Galp" represented "an opportunity to accelerate value creation, but it was never a condition for its delivery."
The merger aimed to combine the downstream businesses of the two companies, creating a unified competitor to marketshare leader Repsol in the Iberian peninsula.
Galp operates the 226,000 barrels/day Sines refinery in Portugal, while Moeve operates two refineries in southwest Spain, at Huelva and Algeciras, with capacities of 220,000 b/d and 240,000 b/d, respectively.
The plan entailed creating an "IndustrialCo" to focus on refining, petrochemicals and supply activities across the three industrial sites, with Moeve becoming the majority partner and Galp taking around a 20% stake. Under the proposed plans, a second business, "RetailCo", was to be co-controlled by Galp and Moeve, holding balanced stakes, combining the oil products sales to direct clients of around 6.5 million mt in 2025 and a combined 3,500 service points between them.
In July, Portuguese Environment and Energy Minister Maria Graca de Carvalho said in a Portuguese parliamentary that the government wanted to ensure "sovereignty" over the country's only refinery at Sines, in the face of the proposed merger.
The government would expect strategic control of the refinery to remain in Portugal, enabling it to provide or guarantee supply in crisis situations, Graca de Carvalho said.
-- Gianluca Baratti
Norway's BW Energy takes Gabon oil asset offline after rig sinks
Borr's Prospector 5 sinks in Dussafu permit
Five workers missing, 110 accounted for
Some 26,000 b/d output temporarily offline
Some 26,000 barrels/day of crude output from BW Energy's Dussafu Marin license off Gabon is temporarily offline after a drilling rig sank over the weekend, leaving as many as five people dead.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

4

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.



Platts Oilgram News

October 26, 2026

In a statement released on its website on Oct. 25, the Norwegian company said an incident occurred on the Prospector 5 rig, owned by Borr Drilling, at 2037 local time on Oct. 24.
"During jacking operations in preparation for a planned drilling campaign, the rig experienced a loss of stability and subsequently sank," the company said. Of the 115 workers onboard, 11 have been accounted for and five are missing.
A search and rescue mission is underway involving BW, rig owner Borr Drilling, and the emergency services, it said.
The company added that production at the MaBoMo facility is "shut down in accordance with standard procedure during ongoing drilling preparations." Dussafu is BW Energy's most important producing asset, responsible for a major share of its daily output.
"There has been no reported damage to the facility or related equipment, nor any accidental discharge of harmful substances to the sea from the facility," it said.
The Dussafu permit pumped 33,200 b/d of crude in 2025, according to the company, with a slew of subsea wells tied back to the MaBoMo platform. Crude is then piped 20 kilometers to the Adolo FPSO for export.
Production in the second quarter of 2026 was approximately 26,000 b/d, according to the partners.
BW Energy holds a 73.5% operating interest in the license, alongside fellow Norwegian producer Panoro at 17.5% and stateowned Gabon Oil Co. at the remaining 9%.
The Oslo-listed company did not respond to a request for comment from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, about the exact production impact or the expected length of the outage.
The company has already started work on its MaBoMo Phase 2 project, which should add 20,000 b/d of production from four new producing wells on the key license and see first oil in Q1 2027, according to BW's Q2 earnings presentation.
Looking further ahead, another two appraisal wells on Dussafu Marin could lead to another 10,000 b/d of output, according to BW.
In a statement of its own, rig owner Borr Drilling said, "preliminary information indicates that the rig experienced a punch through event during preload activities, resulting in loss of stability and the subsequent sinking of the rig".
'We are deeply concerned for those who remain unaccounted for and everyone affected by this incident," said Bruno Morand, Borr's CEO. "Our immediate priority is supporting the ongoing search and rescue efforts and the welfare of all personnel involved."
-- Charlie Mitchell
Equinor, Aker BP collaboration eyes higher Norway oil and gas output
Companies to collaborate on tight reservoirs
NOD sees 2.8 billion boe recoverable potential in such reservoirs
Equinor aims to sustain NCS output into 2035
Norway's Equinor and Aker BP are beginning a strategic collaboration around tight reservoirs aimed at boosting oil

and natural gas development and recovery on the Norwegian Continental Shelf, both said in statements on Oct. 26.
The companies will collaborate on technology development, standardization and knowledge sharing to support work on prospects, discoveries and projects in tight reservoirs across the NCS where oil and gas flow particularly slowly though rock, they said.
The Norwegian Offshore Directorate has estimated some 2.8 billion barrels of oil equivalent in recoverable resource potential from such mapped reservoirs, which include many of the roughly 90 discoveries that have yet to get a development decision, both said.
"The Norwegian Offshore Directorate has therefore encouraged companies to work together," Aker BP said in its statement.
"Scale is the key," Aker BP CEO Karl Johnny Hersvik said. "By considering opportunities across both portfolios, we can create the basis for increased activity, faster learning and lower costs. Together, we can develop solutions more quickly and deploy them at a larger scale. We are starting this work immediately."
Equinor's Executive Vice President for Exploration & Production in Norway, Kjetil Hove, said: "By viewing our portfolios in context and developing common solutions, we can reduce costs and develop and qualify technology faster."
NCS outlook State-owned Equinor aims to sustain production of about
1.3 million barrels of oil equivalent/day on the NCS into 2035. It has estimated that about 70% of this output will come from new wells and developments.
Gas output on the NCS is expected to hold roughly steady over the coming years, thanks to new developments and exploration, before falling toward the end of the decade, the Norwegian Offshore Directorate said in its latest annual report on the NCS in January.
Gas output across the NCS is projected to fall to 107.1 Bcm by 2030, from some 119.8 Bcm in 2025, according to the NOD.
Norway is a key gas supplier for Europe. Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the month-ahead Dutch TTF gas price at 92.78/megawatt-hour on Oct. 23. The index is at its highest point since December 2022.
-- Matt Hoisch
Saudi shipper Bahri expands tanker fleet with $451M VLCC order
Three 320,000 DWT vessels to be delivered by 2029-30
Fleet reaches 107 vessels end of June
Deal brings Bahri's newbuild orders to nine vessels
The National Shipping Co. of Saudi Arabia has signed a $451 million deal with domestic shipbuilder International Maritime Industries Co. to construct and deliver three very large crude carriers, Bahri said in a statement Oct. 25 to the Saudi stock exchange.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

5

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.



Platts Oilgram News

October 26, 2026

The order, signed on Oct. 22, will involve the manufacture of three VLCCs with a capacity of 320,000 deadweight tons each, with delivery to Bahri scheduled in batches between 2029 and 2030, it said in the statement.
Bahri's fleet, which was fully commercially deployed over the second quarter despite the Middle East conflict, stood at "a record" 107 vessels at the end of June 2026, including 49 oil carriers as of the end of the second quarter, the company said July 29 in a quarterly earnings release.
The three vessels will bring the total number of newbuild ships being constructed in Saudi Arabia for Bahri to nine, comprising six dry bulk carriers and three VLCCs, Bahri said.
In October 2025, Bahri signed a deal to purchase six geared Ultramax dry bulk vessels from IMI to be constructed in the shipbuilder's Ras al-Khair shipyard in Saudi Arabia and see delivery between 2028 and 2029, Platts reported Oct. 2, 2025.
Bahri will fund its latest contract through a combination of internal cash resources and banking facilities, according to the statement, while the contract term will end upon the delivery of the last vessel to the company.
The financial impact of the contract will be reflected on Bahri's financial statements after the vessels are received and commercial operations begin, the company said.
Bahri holds a 19.9% equity stake in IMI, a subsidiary of Saudi Arabian Oil Co., while Bahri CEO Ali al-Subaey also serves on IMI's board of directors, according to the company's statement.
-- Lulwa Albuainain
The Americas
US crude oil stocks expected lower amid strong refinery demand
Inventories expected to hit four-week low
Refinery runs likely edge up to 93.6% of capacity
Exports decline as arbitrage economics weaken
US crude oil inventory draws likely extended during the week to Oct. 23, analysts surveyed by Platts, a part of S&P Global Energy, said Oct. 26, against a backdrop of stronger refinery demand.
Commercial crude stocks likely declined 1.2 million barrels to around 411.3 million barrels during the period, analysts said. This fourth straight weekly draw would put stocks at the lowest level since late July and leave them 3.6% behind the seasonal five-year average of US Energy Information Administration data.
Nationwide refinery utilization likely climbed 0.2 percentage point to 93.6% of capacity, analysts said, a five-week high and 6.8% above average for this time of year.
Refinery margins have broadly declined during October but remain robust, S&P Global CERA data showed. The US Gulf Coast WTI MEH cracking margin has averaged $48.72/barrel to-date this month, down from $50.13/b during September.

Distillate cracks remained very strong. The USGC ULSD crack versus WTI MEH has averaged $100.13/b in October compared with $101.03/b in September, CERA data showed.
US distillate stocks likely climbed 600,000 barrels to around 104.9 million barrels during the week to Oct. 23, analysts said, while gasoline stocks were seen up 500,000 barrels over the period at around 205.8 million barrels.
Weekly US crude exports slowed to 3.19 million b/d, according to data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea, falling to the lowest level since early August.
Export arbitrage economics remained supportive along key routes, but have weakened in recent weeks. The incentive for moving WTI MEH into Rotterdam versus North Sea Forties has averaged $14.36/b to date in October, CERA data showed, but averaged just $6.83/b during the week to Oct. 23.
The incentive for exporting Mars crude into China versus Dubai fell to minus $1.57/b during the reporting period, compared with an October-to-date average of $4.76/b, CERA data showed.
-- Christopher Vanmoessner
US lawmakers warn banks, ports against facilitating Russian diesel trade
Lawmakers challenge Russian diesel legality
Tanker carrying Russian fuel heads to NY
Refinery damage constrains export capacity
US lawmakers urged US banks and port authorities not to facilitate Russian diesel transactions and warned of potential criminal and civil liability, challenging the legal basis for the Trump administration's effort to reopen the trade to ease fuel prices.
In an Oct. 24 letter sent to executives of Goldman Sachs, JP MorganChase, Bank of America, Wells Fargo, Morgan Stanley and port authorities in Houston, New York, New Jersey, Beaumont, Texas and Corpus Christi, Texas, US senators and one US representative demanded that the companies preserve records related to proposed or actual Russian diesel transactions and disclose whether they had participated in, or knew of, arrangements to import the fuel into the US.
"Any importation of Russian diesel into the US raises significant legal questions and may be subject to legal action, as well as congressional investigations," US Senators Jeanne Shaheen, Democrat-New Hampshire, Elizabeth Warren, Democrat-Massachusetts, Ron Wyden, Democrat-Oregon, Chris Coons, Democrat-Delaware, Sheldon Whitehouse, DemocratRhode Island, Thom Tillis, Republican-North Carolina and representative and House Foreign Affairs Committee ranking member Gregory Meeks, Democrat-New York, wrote.
The letters followed the Treasury Department's Oct. 9 issuance of General License 135, which allows the sale, delivery and importation of Russian diesel, including into the US, through April 7, 2027. US President Donald Trump announced the exemption alongside commitments to sell additional Russian supplies into American and global markets.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

6

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.



Platts Oilgram News

October 26, 2026

The lawmakers argued that the license does not resolve statutory restrictions on Russian energy imports. Their letter cited the Ending Importation of Russian Oil Act of 2022, which they said generally prohibits such imports unless the president consults Congress and certifies that Russia has ceased military hostilities in Ukraine, poses no immediate military threat to NATO members and recognizes Ukrainians' right to choose their government.
They also cited Section 216 of the Countering America's Adversaries Through Sanctions Act, arguing that the administration had not fulfilled congressional review requirements for licensing actions that significantly alter US foreign policy toward Russia.
The warning extended beyond importers of record. Companies knowingly buying Russian diesel or facilitating its importation could also face criminal penalties, the lawmakers said, urging Goldman Sachs not to support purchases, transportation, unloading, storage, financing or distribution until statutory requirements were satisfied.
The preservation demand covers contracts, shipping and customs documents, payment records, terminal reservations and communications with counterparties and US government officials beginning Sept. 1. The letter does not establish that Goldman Sachs has participated in a transaction.
Russian diesel heads to New York The congressional intervention comes as Russia prepares to
resume diesel shipments to the US. Russia Deputy Prime Minister Alexander Novak said a first tanker was scheduled to depart Russia's Baltic coast Oct. 22, Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, reported Oct. 21. He previously outlined plans to supply 300,000 mt to the US in October and another 500,000 mt in November.
The Malta-flagged medium-sized tanker Agioi Fanendes is heading to New York carrying Russian diesel, according to S&P Global Energy Commodities at Sea data, with the Kremlin expecting deliveries to continue "on a regular basis," Kremlin spokesperson Dimitry Peskov said Oct. 23.
Russia had shipped about 400,000 mt of diesel internationally in October as of the Oct. 21 report, mostly to Egypt, Morocco, Turkey and Brazil, according to CAS data.
Trump's Oct. 9 announcement outlined 4.8 million mt of additional diesel supplies in stages, with the final 3 million mt dependent on the condition of Russian refineries. Russia initially confirmed its willingness to supply petroleum products but did not corroborate the full volume or delivery schedule.
The delivery of these diesel volumes to the US "would only be a displacement of volume from Brazil," a European diesel trader told Platts Oct. 23. "It is a logistical change regarding where the product goes, rather than an increase in flows. We would have to see vastly more flows for it to [affect] the European market."
Supply constraints persist The announcement initially pressured diesel margins. The
NYMEX front-month ULSD crack against WTI fell $6.45/b to

$96.80/b at 1933 GMT Oct. 9, although prices subsequently remained elevated. ICE low sulfur gasoil futures settled at $1,449.50/mt Oct. 20, compared with $1,420.75/mt Oct. 9, according to Platts assessments.
Russia's ability to restore exports remains constrained by refinery damage and domestic supply requirements. A Sept. 30 S&P Global Energy CERA outlook estimated that Ukrainian drone attacks had pushed 3.1 million b/d of Russian refining capacity offline, with third-quarter diesel loadings averaging 140,000 b/d, down 500,000 b/d year over year.
Novak said Russia could restore full diesel export volumes by year-end. CERA's September outlook was more cautious, forecasting that continuing attacks and domestic demand would prevent a return to pre-crisis exports by year-end and keep supplies constrained through 2027.

Brazil's PPSA to sell 4.5 million barrels from Buzios subsalt oil field in latest spot sale
PPSA schedules Oct. 28 auction
Offloads set for December
Subsalt production grows
PPSA plans to sell 4.5 million barrels of crude from the Buzios production-sharing field Oct. 28 at the regulator's latest spot market auction, the Brazilian government subsalt management company said Oct. 26.
The offered cargoes, 500,000-1 million barrels each, will be available for offload in December, PPSA said. Five lots will be available at the sale, which is PPSA's 10th auction overall, and includes barrels that failed to sell at recent spot market auctions.
The first lot will feature a 1-million-barrel cargo from the floating production, storage, and offloading vessel P-79, which will be offloaded Dec. 9-10, PPSA said. The second lot will cover a 1-million-barrel cargo from the FPSO Almirante Tamandare, which will also be offloaded Dec. 9-10.
The auction's third and fourth lots cover three smaller cargoes of 500,000 barrels each, PPSA said. The FPSO P-75 will have 500,000 barrels available Dec. 10-11, while the FPSO P-77 will have 500,000 barrels available Dec. 11-12. The FPSO P-76, meanwhile, will have a 500,000-barrel cargo available Dec. 22-23.
The fifth lot covers a 1-million-barrel cargo from the FSPO Almirante Barroso for offload Dec. 29-30, PPSA said.
The oil volumes failed to sell at PPSA's ninth spot market auction held Oct. 7, according to PPSA. The regulator has found that bidding at recent spot market auctions has been undermined by volatile international oil and refined product prices, despite growing output from subsalt production-sharing fields and strong global demand for secure energy supplies.
PPSA put 4 million barrels of crude from Buzios up for sale at a spot market auction Oct. 7 and failed to generate a single offer, according to the regulator. TotalEnergies, meanwhile, claimed a 500,000-barrel cargo at a spot sale held Oct. 13 and exchanged

Ovintiv acquires Canadian light oil producer Logan Energy for C$1.26 billion
Montney assets produce 20,000 boe/d
Deal includes 24,000 b/d crude storage
Second major deal after NuVista Energy
Ovintiv Inc. unveiled a definitive agreement to buy Calgarybased light oil producer Logan Energy Corp. under a C$1.26 billion ($890 million) deal, further extending its footprint in the Western Canadian Sedimentary Basin's shale resources, it said Oct. 26.
The acquisition will add nearly 20,000 barrels/day of oil equivalent production, including 7,000 b/d of crude oil and condensate, 70 million cubic feet/day of natural gas, and over 1 million b/d of other NGLs such as C2 (ethane) and C4 (butane), Ovintiv said in a release.
The deal also provides the company access to 420 net 10,000-foot-equivalent oil well locations (including 220 premium well locations at an average cost of $1.5 million per premium location) and about 266,000 net acres of land position in the Montney oil window, it said, noting some 80% of those acres are undeveloped.
Logan's assets are located in the Simonette and Pouce Coupe areas, which are in close proximity to Ovintiv's existing Montney

operations and also include two new oil batteries and additional natural gas processing capacity, it said.
With the deal, Ovintiv's core land position in the Montney is expected to increase to nearly 720,000 net acres, the release said, adding that 60,000 net acres with exposure to the Duvernay shale play are also included in the transaction.
Logan has 100% ownership in two new crude oil batteries (storage facilities) in Simonette, with a total capacity of 24,000 b/d, the first of which came online during the third quarter of 2026, and the second is expected to come online by the end of the first quarter of 2027, the Ovintiv release said.
Logan also has 50% ownership at a natural gas processing facility in Simonette with a total capacity of 90 million cubic feet/ day and 65% ownership in a natural gas processing facility in Pouce Coupe with a total capacity of 57 MMcf/d, it said.
Ownership in these facilities is expected to enhance Ovintiv's Montney margins and provide future growth optionality, it said.
"This bolt-on transaction delivers value for our shareholders by adding high-margin oil and condensate production and infrastructure, utilizing our operational advantage to drive significant synergies, and adding to our already deep drilling inventory and future growth optionality," Ovintiv CEO Brendan McCracken said in a statement.
"The Logan assets have demonstrated leading oil productivity performance and will immediately compete for capital alongside the rest of our portfolio," McCracken said.
Ovintiv's prime areas of activity are in the Permian Basin in Texas and Montney in the WCSB, with the company's second quarter 2026 production averaging 615,000 boe/d that included 206,000 b/d of crude oil and condensate, 82,000 b/d of other NGLs and 2 billion cubic feet/day of natural gas, it said late July in its earnings release.
Building WCSB inventory In late August, Ovtintiv said it was expanding its Montney
position by acquiring about 20,000 net acres and 120 total well locations in the liquids-rich Alberta oil window for $230 million.
Also, in late 2025, the company said it had a definitive agreement - valued at $2.7 billion - to acquire another Calgarybased producer, NuVista Energy, with oil, condensate, and natural gas assets and production in the Montney play.
The acquisition added 930 net 10,000-foot equivalent well locations, and 140,000 net acres (about 70% undeveloped) in the core of the oil-rich Alberta Montney play, Ovintiv said then, noting full-year 2026 production from the acquired assets is expected to average nearly 100,000 boe/d with 25,000 b/d of oil and condensate.
The assets are directly adjacent to Ovintiv's current operations and include access to processing and downstream infrastructure with significant available capacity, it said, with the company gaining access to drilling inventory at an average cost of $1.3 million per location.
-- Ashok Dutta

Plans to expand Brazil's domestic refining capacity will also likely be maintained under the ongoing Lula administration, with Petrobras CEO Magda Chambriard confirming in September that the company wants to meet 100% of the country's diesel demand under the upcoming 2027-2031 investment plan. The plan is expected to be released by the end of November.
Lula won 51.79% of the vote in a second-round runoff against challenger Flavio Bolsonaro, who received 48.21%. The final tally represented a political turnaround for Lula, who trailed Bolsonaro in the first round held Oct. 4 at 45.16%. Bolsonaro, the son of former president Jair Bolsonaro, led the first round at 47.03%.
The election outcome caught financial markets by surprise, with investors having largely anticipated a victory by Bolsonaro, according to Oviedo.
The initial negative market reaction reflected that unexpected result, but investors would eventually adjust their expectations, Oviedo said.
Record production, exploration success Lula's reelection also came amid a string of production
records from subsalt production sharing fields discovered during his first two terms. Chambriard has accelerated the installation of floating production units, with five pumping first oil from the Buzios and Mero fields since October 2024.
Brazil's oil output set a third-consecutive monthly production record in August at 4.615 million b/d, topping the previous monthly record of 4.498 million b/d set in July, the National Petroleum Agency, or ANP, said Oct. 1. Subsalt fields accounted for 82.6% of the country's total oil and gas output at 3.842 million b/d and 177.6 million cubic meters/d, or 4.959 million boe/d, from 203 wells.
Brazil's ability to attract international oil companies reflects not only its substantial prospective resources but also the governance framework established through reforms at Petrobras, Oviedo said.
That framework has helped make Petrobras a reliable partner for international investors, distinguishing Brazil from other regional producers such as Mexico's Pemex, where government policy exerts much more direct influence over corporate decisions, he said.
Petrobras also officially opened Brazil's equatorial margin, widely considered one of the country's last two remaining exploration frontiers. Petrobras made two separate oil discoveries in the Morpho well drilled in the Foz do Amazonas Basin. That was the first major exploration well in the region in more than 15 years.
While Petrobras received an amended license to drill three additional wells, the company still remains waiting on environmental licenses to tap other exploration wells in a region that geologists believe holds similar oil and natural gas deposits as those found in Guyana, Ghana and Suriname. The Lula administration has backed exploration in the region in an effort to rebuild Brazil's oil and gas reserves as the subsalt

region matures. Brazil's subsalt oil and gas production is expected to peak
in the early to mid-2030s, with additional exploration needed to mitigate expected future declines. The expected downturn has raised concerns that Brazil could be forced to return to the ranks of net oil importers for the first time since 2014.
Brazil is expected to continue holding near-annual Open Acreage concession and production sharing bid rounds after updating rules

Alberta backs bill to remove crude tanker ban in Canadian Pacific
Bill scheduled for debate on Oct. 28
Sees options for crude-by-rail exports
Alberta has extended its support for a private bill in the Canadian Parliament to repeal the federal Oil Tanker Moratorium Act, which is scheduled for debate on Oct. 28, a provincial government statement said Oct. 26.
The provincial government supports private member's Bill C-264, and repealing the ban would open the door to crude-byrail exports through Prince Rupert in coastal British Columbia, giving the province another route to reach customers around the world, Premier Danielle Smith said in a statement.
"Our oil to the United States is selling at a significant discount and at a disadvantage to the entire country [Canada]," Smith said. "The only effective way to close that gap is to increase exports off our western coast and get more customers competing for our energy. For decades, limited access to tidewater has left Alberta heavily dependent on a single customer, putting our oil at a competitive disadvantage and reducing the value of our resources."
In March, Conservative Member of Parliament David McKenzie from the Calgary Signal Hill riding in Alberta first tabled the bill for discussion in parliament.
The Conservative Party of Canada is the official opposition in parliament (House of Commons) with 137 members, with a total of 342 seats.
"The tanker ban remains a stumbling block to accessing Asia, one of the fastest-growing markets in the world," Alberta's Energy and Minerals Minister Brian Jean said in a statement. "Sailing from Prince Rupert would provide the shortest energy shipping route to Asia. This means lower shipping costs, less fuel used and fewer emissions created. Producers have already expressed interest in using this route for crude oil exports."
In June 2019, the federal government enacted the Oil Moratorium Act, which banned the movement of tankers laden with over 12,500 metric tons (91,625 barrels) of crude oil in the area from the Canada/US border in the north, down to the point on British Columbia's mainland across from the northern tip of Vancouver Island. It also included Haida Gwaii and the waters of

"We safely manage tanker navigation in busy and difficult waters on both the west and east coasts without incident. We can also do so with oil tankers carrying Alberta oil," Jean said.

OIL FUTURES: Crude pulls back from recent highs despite continued Middle East tensions
Six VLCCs exit Strait of Hormuz
Iran launches 29 attacks on ships this month: IMO
1.5 billion crude barrels exited Hormuz since summer
Crude oil futures settled lower Oct. 26 as traders booked profits against a backdrop of continued Strait of Hormuz vessel flows and persistent Middle East escalation risks.
NYMEX December WTI settled $1.51/barrel lower at $101.76/b and ICE December Brent declined 91 cents/b to $115.81/b.
"The pullback comes despite continued Houthi attacks on Saudi Arabian infrastructure over the weekend, suggesting some technical profit-taking after last week's sharp upswing," Priyanka Sachdeva, Head of Market Insights from Phillip Nova, said in a note Oct. 26. Neither Aramco nor the Saudi government acknowledged the attacks.
Strait of Hormuz vessel transits were steady at 17 on Oct. 25 and included six VLCC movements, S&P Global Commodities at Sea said in an Oct. 26 report, despite continued attacks on shipping.
Iran has launched its heaviest wave of attacks on merchant ships around Hormuz and in the wider region this month, with the International Maritime Organization recording 29 attacks during Oct. 1-22, exceeding all monthly tallies since March.
"The threat is no longer one route or one front: Iran, the Houthis in Yemen and militias in Iraq can all put Middle East oil infrastructure at risk," according to the latest crude oil market outlook from S&P Global Energy CERA.
NYMEX November RBOB climbed 69 points/gallon to $3.2185/ gal and November ULSD ended the session down 7.22 cents/gal at $4.9120/gal.
US forces on Oct. 25 marked the export of at least 1.5 billion barrels of crude oil since the summer through Hormuz, with US Central Command commander Admiral Brad Cooper flying over the chokepoint in a US Army CH-47 Chinook, Central Command said on X. It noted that more than 3,000 commercial ships had successfully transited Hormuz with support from CENTCOM forces over the last six months.

VLCC transits climb at Hormuz; steady at Bab al-Mandab for second day
Hormuz sees six VLCCs, most since Oct 17
Bab al-Mandab records one VLCC for two days
US marks 1.5 billion barrels through Hormuz
The Strait of Hormuz had six VLCC transits on Oct. 25, the most since Oct. 17, while the Bab al-Mandab Strait recorded one VLCC on both Oct. 24 and Oct. 25, S&P Global Commodities at Sea said in an Oct. 26 report.
Hormuz traffic in total was steady at 17 ships on Oct. 24 and Oct. 25, while Bab al-Mandab transits jumped to 42 ships from 27 ships over the same period, according to the CAS report. The VLCC total for Hormuz on Oct. 25 was up from five on Oct. 24, CAS data showed.
The outbound VLCC from the Persian Gulf was Monaco Prosperity, while inbound VLCCs were Bangladesh Prosperity, Ennie, Thailand Prosperity, West Loyalty and Bulgaria Prosperity, CAS said. The VLCCs that transited Bab al-Mandab were Della, heading northbound, on Oct. 25 and Karina, also moving northbound, on Oct. 24, according to CAS.
US forces on Oct. 25 marked the export of at least 1.5 billion barrels of crude oil since the summer through Hormuz, with US Central Command commander Admiral Brad Cooper flying over the chokepoint in a US Army CH-47 Chinook, Central Command said on X. It noted that more than 3,000 commercial ships had successfully transited Hormuz with support from CENTCOM forces over the last six months.
"Today, primary transit routes in the Strait of Hormuz remain free and clear of Iranian sea mines thanks to incredible efforts by our people," Cooper said. "We remain intensely focused on keeping commercial traffic flowing as we work with our teammates in the US interagency, Gulf Cooperation Council, and shipping industry."
The comment came despite Iran launching its heaviest wave of attacks on merchant ships around Hormuz and in the wider region this month, with the International Maritime Organization recording 29 attacks during Oct. 1-22, exceeding all monthly tallies since March.
On Oct. 26, the UK Maritime Trade Operations Centre reported a tanker exiting Hormuz was disabled after being struck by an unknown projectile Oct. 23. No crew casualties or oil spills have been reported.
Of the 16 VLCC crossings identified over Oct. 23-25, 10 were assessed as inbound and six outbound, CAS said. Inbound VLCC movements included five of South Korea's Sinokor-operated ships, and the Saudi-linked Majra and Qasba. The VLCC Ennie moved into the Persian Gulf after last loading a cargo of Basrah Heavy crude in early October, and China-owned VLCC Indigo Ray also moved inbound, CAS said.
Outbound transits included Sinokor-operated Romania Prosperity, UAE-linked Das and Al Faya, and Saudi-linked Kahla and Emerald Shine, CAS said.

Water levels on Rhine rise, but relief forecast to be short-lived
Rhine water levels reach 56 cm at Kaub
Switzerland releases emergency diesel stocks
Freight costs remain elevated at 255/mt
Water levels on the Rhine, a critical European inland waterway for freight, rose above 50 centimeters Oct. 26 and are forecast to hover around 70 cm in the next few days, but the rebound in flows is expected to provide only short-term relief to fuel supply chains across the region, according to market participants.
Water levels at Kaub, the shallowest part of the Middle Rhine, stood at 56 cm as of late afternoon Oct. 26 and are forecast to exceed 70 cm by the end of the day, then expected to hold around that level for the following three days, according to data from the German water authority WSV.
The Rhine water levels exceeded 50 cm on Oct. 26 for the first time since Sept. 4, with levels mostly below 10 cm from Sept. 24 to Oct. 10 and mostly below 20 cm from Oct. 11 to Oct. 22.
The recent rise in Rhine water levels has generated renewed market activity, but the improvement is expected to be short-lived,

Germany is comparatively less affected, according to the market participant, because of its larger refining base and less severe isolation from Rhine supply routes, although low water levels continue to restrict river transportation.
That said, rail options in Germany remain limited due to the ongoing closure for refurbishment of the right-bank Middle Rhine Line, a key German freight route. The line is expected to stay closed until Dec. 12.
"The situation for refiners along the Rhine remains broadly unchanged in October, with the month-to-date average water level at Kaub at around 14 cm. Crude supply remains relatively stable, but the main logistical challenge is clearing refined products amid higher freight costs due to highly limited options," said Sammir Lesage, analyst at S&P Global Energy CERA.
"Those additional costs are likely to be passed through the supply chain, with consumers ultimately bearing much of the burden", Lesage added.

SABIC expects `gradual recovery' in petchems market on urea, S-Oil, Fujian growth prospects
SABIC completes thermoplastics divestment sale
Petrochemicals prices fall 10% in third quarter
Overall sales volumes at 80% of "pre-crisis levels": CEO
SABIC is looking at a "gradual recovery" in the market on growth projects that include urea, South Korea's S-Oil and the Fujian project in China, it said in an earnings statement Oct. 25.
SABIC, the Middle East's largest petrochemicals producer, said it completed the divestment of its engineering thermoplastics business in the Americas and Europe to German private equity company Mutares SE & Co. KGaA during the third quarter.
Petrochemical prices dropped 10% from the second quarter, "as buyers remain cautious amid persistent uncertainties," while the sales volume climbed 24% to 6.71 million metric tons, it said.
A gradual recovery in petrochemicals is expected as "supply/ demand conditions rebalance," SABIC said in the statement.
Growth projects include the Fujian petrochemicals complex, with three core units completing successful trial runs, SABIC said.
SABIC CEO Faisal al-Faqeer said on an Oct. 26 company earnings call that this positions the company "for full start-up during the fourth quarter of 2026."
The company is planning a 54% increase in urea production capacity, while 1.8 million mt of annual methanol production capacity will go forward after a feedstock allocation, SABIC said. It also noted a 1.2 million mt/year polyethylene marketing agreement with South Korea's S-Oil Corp., majority owned by Saudi Aramco, which also controls SABIC.
Rerouted shipments Amid regional disruptions, SABIC "activated alternative
shipping routes and logistic arrangements," the company said. Faqeer said on the investor's earnings call that these included the addition of "another export outlet," joining "the recently established routes" of Yanbu, Rabigh and Jeddah."
"Vessel lifting increased by 38% quarter on quarter," Faqeer said, as SABIC is making use of both shipping via the kingdom's Red Sea ports as well as "taking advantage of safe passage whenever available through the Strait of Hormuz," Faqeer said.
"We also increased polymer volumes shuttling from Jubail to

the west coast ports by more than 230% compared with the precrisis levels," Faqeer said, attributing this, among other logistical decisions, to the company's restoration of overall sales volumes "to around 80% of pre-crisis levels," Faqeer said.
Over the second quarter, the company transported 150% more polymer volumes from eastern facilities to western ports as shipments were rerouted over the period, SABIC said in its July 29 quarterly results statement.
Despite shipping disruptions through the Strait of Hormuz and the Bab al-Mandab Strait, SABIC managed to complete the firstever urea shipment through the Neom port on the Red Sea, the company said.
"It's still too early to talk about quarter four, but we continue to push our volumes through all available routes, and we are taking all the learnings from all the quarters in this year during the crisis to continue to deliver [in the] fourth quarter," Faqeer said.
The company "optimized feedstock utilization leveraging ethylene network flexibility in Jubail," according to the Oct. 25 statement. Polymers sales in Q3 rose 23% from Q2, chemicals sales climbed 24% over the same period and agriculture nutrients sales increased 68% quarter over quarter in Q3, it said.

Singapore christens first ammonia-fueled commercial cargo vessel
93,000-cu m carrier completes construction
Fatures Everllence's first commercial ammonia dual-fuel engine

The Maritime and Port Authority of Singapore and Eastern Pacific Shipping have marked the christening of the ammonia dual-fueled vessel Terra Clove at HD Hyundai Heavy Industries (HHI) in Ulsan, South Korea, according to a joint statement published Oct. 22.
The 93,000-cubic-meter Very Large Ammonia Carrier is EPS' first ammonia dual-fueled vessel and the first ammonia dualfueled commercial newbuild to be registered under the Singapore Registry of Ships.
Terra Clove is equipped with Everllence's first commercial ammonia dual-fueled two-stroke marine engine, which completed factory acceptance testing in April 2026.
The vessel is designed to transport ammonia as cargo while also using ammonia as a propulsion fuel, and incorporates specialized fuel handling, containment and safety systems.

-- Shu qi Ng
US steps up African oil and gas spending amid fossil fuel pivot
US DFC invests $14 billion in Africa, focusing on equity
Capex for African projects up 70% from previous year
IOCs welcome 'more attractive investment environment'
The US is taking on a growing role in funding African oil and gas projects as it has shifted its support for green energy and rejected forecasts of fossil fuel demand reaching a plateau, according to government officials, analysts and private investors.
A lack of Western funding from climate-conscious lenders is often cited by Africa's oil and gas advocates as producing an unfair disadvantage for the continent, where many countries lack the financing to develop their natural resources.
However, the policy shift under US President Donald Trump's second term appears to be directing new attention to Africa, both from state entities and the US oil majors betting on sustained demand growth for the world's hydrocarbons.
In a sign of its new focus on partnerships to develop African energy and mineral resources, the US sent its largest-ever delegation to Cape Town's African Energy Week from Oct. 12-16, Joshua Volz, a special envoy for the US Department of Energy, said at the event.
Now in its fifth year, the conference gathers a variety of African energy investors, ranging from international energy

companies to delegations from Russia, China and the Middle East -- all regions that have assumed new prominence amid a Western withdrawal.
Spending rebound According to analysis from S&P Global Energy CERA, capital
expenditure on African exploration and production peaked at $57 billion in 2013 before retreating to $20 billion in 2021, largely due to IOCs, including Chevron and ExxonMobil, slashing spending.
However, from its floor in 2025, investment has rebounded strongly in 2026. In the year to date, Africa has already attracted 70% more funding, marking the largest annual increase in more than two decades.
Addressing delegates in Cape Town, Volz emphasized a renewed appetite from the White House "double down on commercial opportunities" in Africa, which, at $8 billion, is already responsible for the largest share of spending from the US ExportImport Bank.
Increasingly, the US Development Finance Corporation, a federal government agency focused on advancing its foreign policy and economic development, is also shifting its focus from debt to equity positions in Africa, where it has $14 billion invested, Volz said.
Among the assets under consideration by the DFC are those owned by Russian energy giant Lukoil, according to reporting from the Financial Times, which wrote that the agency is part of a consortium with US businessman Todd Boehly and several Gulf investors bidding for its international portfolio.
Lukoil, which was sanctioned by the US in 2025, has a significant African oil and gas footprint spanning Egypt, Cameroon, Nigeria, Ghana and the Republic of Congo. A DFC official declined to confirm its involvement because of the project's commercial sensitivity.
Industry response The US pivot has been interpreted by some as a green light for
an African spending spree. "The transition might be dead, and if I have a chance, I want
to kill it in Africa," said NJ Ayuk, executive chairman of the African Energy Chamber, in his opening address to the group's conference in Cape Town.
Borrowing from a favored Trump slogan, the African lobby group also launched a "Drill Baby Drill" wine range to coincide with its event, underscoring potential synergies between the White House and the continent's oil producers.
The shift in government stance has had ripple effects beyond the public sector. JJ Enoch, a senior associate at geopolitical risk advisory firm JS Held, said "a meaningful shift" in White House support could improve IOC confidence in the long-term policy environment and draw private funding into state-backed projects.
Chevron, which currently produces roughly 300,000 b/d from sub-Saharan Africa, has increased its acreage position by around 70% in recent years, said Brent Gros, the company's head of offshore exploration, at the African Energy Week event.

Platts Oilgram News

October 26, 2026

Noting key milestones such as the approval of the company's offshore Aseng gas project in Equatorial Guinea, Gros heralded a "more attractive investment environment" in Africa that could translate to stronger growth.
"We've had some projects that, frankly, have been stranded for about a decade, and you start to see momentum with those in a really positive way," he said.
ExxonMobil has also bet big on Nigerian deepwater projects with a $35 billion investment and has highlighted new infill drilling on its 20-year-old Block 15 asset in Angola as a sign of its African production ambitions. "Every day we're looking to grow it. I think the future is very bright," said Hunter Farris, the company's senior vice president for deepwater.
-- Kelly Norways
UK floats fast-track approvals for `urgent' oil, gas tiebacks ...from page 1

provided before the legislation is introduced could herald a shift in approach from Burnham's government, amid rising energy security concerns in the UK. Industry insiders say modern horizontal drilling techniques allow for hydrocarbon exploitation at considerable distances from existing fields.
The new prime minister has signaled he would take a more pragmatic approach than his predecessor Keir Starmer, who angered industry players by extending and increasing the unpopular Energy Profts Levy, which has raised the headline tax rate to 78% in the North Sea.
The EPL and bans on new drilling have been blamed for a wave of IOC exits and consolidation deals on the UK Continental Shelf, as well as a drop in national oil and gas production. It is set to be replaced with a less stringent Oil and Gas Price Mechanism in or before 2030, according to officials.
New energy secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh is yet to make a decision on the 70,000 b/d Rosebank oil and gas field, which like Jackdaw has been subject to legal challenges.
Failure to approve it could make the UK oil sector uninvestable, according to Rosebank stakeholder Equinor.
UK crude output has fallen by about two-thirds since 2009 to roughly 600,000 b/d, according to official figures. The country's Brent and Forties crude grades underpin the Platts Dated Brent benchmark, last assessed at $126.69/b on Oct. 23. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.
-- Charlie Mitchell, Matt Hoisch

Platts Oilgram News

October 26, 2026

September milder in Europe, but colder in Canada and Japan

September was milder in Europe, but colder in Canada and Japan than a year ago, according to data obtained from CustomWeather and gathered by Platts.

A total of 19 cities geographically representative of Europe had 26.1% fewer heating degree days in September than in September 2025. The total for the SeptemberSeptember period was 26.1% fewer degree days than the similar period last year and 35.1% fewer than normal.

Eight cities geographically representative of Canada had 48% more degree days in September than in September 2025. The total for the September-September period was 48% more than the similar period last year and 4.7% fewer than normal.

Four cities geographically representative of Japan had 45.9% more degree days in September than in September 2025. The total for the September-September period was 45.9% more than the similar period last year and 27.5% fewer than normal.

Platts Oilgram Price Report
Volume 104 / Issue 204 / October 23, 2026

News

Market analysis

OIL FUTURES: ICE Brent highest since April on Middle East supply risks
ICE December Brent tops $116/b Hormuz transits climb to 23 on Oct. 22
Prompt-dated ICE Brent settled at the highest level since late April Oct. 23, as geopolitical risk premiums mounted amid concerns that escalating Middle East tensions could impact supply.
ICE December Brent climbed $3.55/b to settle at $116.72/b, the highest level since April 29, while NYMEX December WTI ended the session up $3.80/b at $103.27/b.
"We continue to ascribe rising crude oil prices to inventory depletion and escalation risk, and we retain our upside bias," Clearview Energy Partners analysts said in an Oct. 23 note. "The prospect of kinetic engagement deferred until after the election has loomed since August, but traders may be pricing it in more aggressively as polling in Israel and the US grows closer."
Middle Eastern oil exports have recovered to nearly 75% of prewar levels during the first half of October, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea, but a regional escalation would likely impact these flows.
"Tankers have been attacked, there have been reports of mines near the Strait of Hormuz, Saudi facilities have been hit, embassy personnel have been moved and the market is still digesting the possibility of renewed US-Israeli strikes on Iran after the midterms," Quintex global strategist Stephen Innes said in an Oct. 23 note.
NYMEX November RBOB settled 3.51 cents/gallon higher at $3.2116/gal and November ULSD climbed 5.21 cents/gal to $4.9842/gal.
(continued on page 36)

International Crude
FACTBOX: Houthi attacks on Saudi oil, gas assets fuel price risk premium
Brent futures top $116/b as Houthis claim fresh strikes
Yanbu, East-West Pipeline critical amid Hormuz disruption
Saudi crude rerouting north via Suez as Red Sea risks climb
Oil prices have climbed as renewed Houthi attacks raise risks to Saudi Arabia's oil infrastructure and the export routes the kingdom has increasingly relied on amid disruption to shipping through the Strait of Hormuz.
Saudi crude continues to reach international markets, including via Yanbu, the Suez Canal and Egypt's SUMED pipeline, but the latest attacks have put the market on edge over both production infrastructure and alternative export routes out of the Middle East.
"The threat is no longer one route or one front: Iran, the Houthis in Yemen and militias in Iraq can all put Middle East oil infrastructure at risk," according to the latest crude oil market outlook from S&P Global CERA.
The following are key facts about the attacks on Saudi energy infrastructure and their market impact:
Infrastructure Repeated Houthi strikes have targeted Saudi Arabia's core
production and export facilities, raising fears of disruption to crude flows.
Yemen-based Houthis on Oct. 22 claimed an attack on Saudi Aramco facilities in Yanbu and said a fire broke out at the targeted site. Saudi authorities and Aramco have not confirmed an attack or damage as of Oct. 23. Aramco did not respond to a request for comment Oct. 22.
Yanbu is home to Aramco's 400,000 barrels/day Samref refinery, its 400,000 b/d Yasref refinery and the 228,000 b/d Yanbu refinery, as well as crude export infrastructure.
Saudi Arabia's East-West Pipeline, which is critical for rerouting crude exports toward Saudi Arabia's Red Sea port of Yanbu by bypassing the Strait of Hormuz, was shut following Sept. 10 drone attacks. In early October, flow rates through the 7 million b/d capacity pipeline had climbed to 5.8 million b/d after repairs had been carried out, Saudi energy minister Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman said.
(continued on page 3)

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 23, 2026

The SUMED Pipeline in Egypt carries up to 2.5 million b/d northbound from the Red Sea to the Mediterranean, supporting Saudi crude flows toward European and other markets.
On Oct. 19, the Houthis said they had targeted Aramco facilities in Yanbu and the Shaybah oil field with "ballistic and cruise missiles and drones," adding that fires had erupted at the sites. A few days prior, Houthis attacked Aramco sites in Dhahran, home to the company's headquarters, and in Riyadh, as well as an Aramco administrative building.
Trade flows Shipping attacks have forced sharp declines in regional
exports, prompting Aramco to reroute barrels and reassure Asian buyers. With the recent rise in attacks on shipping, Middle East crude
exports have averaged 11.9 million b/d so far in October, down from 15 million b/d in September, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea. Aramco has committed at least 24 million barrels of crude for ship-to-ship transfers off Sohar, Oman, to Asian customers in September and October. The increase in traffic through Suez "closely coincides with escalating Houthi attacks targeting Saudi shipping near the Bab al-Mandab strait," CAS said in a Oct. 20 report. The Houthis declared a naval blockade on Saudi shipping on July 20.

Aramco had shifted crude flows to Yanbu on the Red Sea after shipping disruptions through the Strait of Hormuz curtailed exports from its east coast terminals.
Saudi crude shipments moving north through the Suez Canal reached about 653,000 b/d over Oct. 1-19, according to CAS data, accounting for the majority of northbound crude flows.
Saudi Arabia's visible gross crude loadings have rebounded sharply in October, as Yanbu exports recovered following a pipeline attack that disrupted flows in September, according to CAS data Oct. 19.
In its Oct. 14 monthly report, the International Energy Agency said Saudi Arabian crude supply in September had surged 2.3 million b/d month over month to 8.3 million b/d, the highest level since February, as production recovered from multiple attacks on oil infrastructure.
Prices Crude benchmarks have jumped on fears that further attacks
could disrupt Saudi supply, with Platts assessments capturing the premium buyers are paying.
ICE December Brent crude futures rose above $116/b at 14:25 GMT Oct. 23, up 2.5% from the previous close, while the NYMEX December light sweet crude contract was up 2.7% at $102.19/b.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Dated Brent at $122.44/b on Oct. 22, up 2.21% day over day.
Aramco set the Arab Light official selling price to Asia for September at $111.89/b, up 25% from the August OSP.
The VLCC rate for shipping 270,000 metric tons of crude from Yanbu to the Far East was assessed at $198.63/mt Oct. 22, down 16.7% month over month but up sharply from $36.42/mt on July 20, according to Platts data.
Platts assessed the Sidi Kerir-Far East VLCC rate at $230.77/ mt on Oct. 22, up 24.6% from $185.19/mt on Sept. 25 when the assessment began.
The LR1 rate for transporting 55,000 mt of clean petroleum products from the Red Sea to Japan was assessed at $250.80/mt on Oct. 22, up from $186.85/mt on Sept. 22 and $50.16/mt on July 20.
-- Lauren Holtmeier
CPC Blend discount to Urals falls to 6-year low as sweet differentials correct
Urals prices stabilize amid balanced sour complex
CPC Blend differentials fall on weak demand
The discount paid for Kazakh CPC Blend FOB Novorossiysk over Russian Urals fell to its lowest level in over six years on Oct. 23, as the Mediterranean sour complex remains balanced while sweet crude differentials experience a sharp correction.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

3

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.



Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 23, 2026

Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Urals FOB Novorossiysk at $25.35/barrel discount to Dated Brent Oct. 23, over $6/b above CPC Blend FOB Novorossiysk, which was at minus $31.90/b as high freight, limited demand and increased MOC offering depressed the differentials for the flagship Kazakh grade. The spread is the lowest since April 28, 2020, when Platts assessed it at minus $7.585/b.
The spread between sour Urals and sweet CPC has been narrowing since late September but has flipped only in recent days as CPC's prices for the November trading cycle corrected.
This comes as the sour crude complex in the Mediterranean and Black Sea remains balanced amid stable flows from the Middle East and improvements in flows from Saudi Arabia.
"There is oil, I don't see scarcity at the moment," a Mediterranean trader said.

With the market past the disruption caused in September by the attack on the East-West pipeline, sour crude differentials have stabilized with Basrah Medium heard offered flat to November average Dated Brent for the past week and Kirkuk valued at a small premium, according to heard values published by Platts.
Market participants also commented on the improved availability of Saudi crude as exports rebound, but these additional flows are yet to push sour differentials significantly lower as most of the correction in Mediterranean sours took place in late September and early October.
Urals price DAP India has stabilized throughout October at around a $3/b premium to the calendar month average Dated Brent, as improved sour supply from the Middle East has forced Russian crude to price more competitively.
Meanwhile, the Mediterranean sweets corrected heavily over the last week, pushing Azeri Light and then CPC Blend a few dollars lower in a matter of days as traders struggled to clear cargoes loading in early November.
CPC Blend CIF Augusta differential, which averaged $0.93/b in September, fell from $1.76/b Oct. 15 to minus $6.855/b Oct. 22, Platts data showed. For the delivered Suezmax cargoes, the price reached its lowest level since the assessment was launched on June 2, 2025.
The correction in CPC prices is part of a broader trend in the Mediterranean sweet market, but the fall in delivered prices has been particularly sharp.
"The market seems pretty depressed at the moment, but sometimes it is just sentiment, in addition to freight and structure, of course," a second Med trader said.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 23, 2026

With differentials declining rapidly, market participants found it hard to make predictions about future prices, as they focused on day-to-day moves.
"It's like trying to catch a falling knife," a third Med trader said. -- Jan Kedzior
CPC Blend FOB differentials sink to all-time lows amid pricey freight, ample supply
CIF Augusta price at lowest in nearly four years
Black Sea-Med freight firmly above w1,000
CPC searching for levels that open arbitrages
The FOB differential of Aframax CPC Blend crude oil has fallen to its lowest level on record as high freight costs and rising insurance premiums depress differentials for the flagship Kazakh grade.
Platts assessed CPC Blend FOB Aframax differential at a $28.87/b discount to Dated Brent on Oct. 22, the lowest since the assessment was launched on Nov. 1, 2011, while the price for CPC Blend delivered CIF Augusta fell to minus $6.855/b, the lowest since Dec. 20, 2022.
The FOB price, calculated as a netback from the CIF price using Black Sea-Med freight, Additional War Risk Premium and cost of delays in the Turkish Straits, has been depressed in recent weeks as the market searched for levels which would allow some of the CPC barrels to leave the region.
The loading program, which was reported at around 1.7 million b/d for October and is expected to be broadly similar in November, is too large to be absorbed by the Mediterranean market and relies on exports to Northwest Europe and Asia to clear monthly volumes.
While elevated freight costs have impacted across the crude complex, the situation for Black Sea barrels is particularly acute with Black Sea-Med freight firmly above 1,000 Worldscale points since Oct. 9, compared with around w800 for crossMediterranean routes.
Added to that is the Additional War Risk Premium charged to charterers operating in high-risk areas, which has recently spiked in the Black Sea to around $5/b, up from an average of $2.87/b in September.
"The freight is too high, it's nonsense," one Black Sea trader said.
But despite high transport costs, the market remains very well supplied, and in the absence of particularly strong refining demand, sellers need to compete on price to clear their volumes.
"It is not just sentiment, it is fundamentals - there is a lot of oil," one Mediterranean source said.
Activity in the Platts Market on Close assessment process has also picked up in recent days, driving prices lower every day.
On Oct. 19, Vitol and Chevron offered cargoes delivered Augusta loading Nov. 8-12 and reached a 15 cents/b premium to Dated Brent. The following day the offers were outstanding at minus $2.35/b, then on Oct. 22 BP lifted Vitol's offer at minus

$7/b while Chevron's indication was outstanding at London close at minus $7.10/b.
"There is too much CPC in the Mediterranean," a second Med source said. "It must keep getting lower until it reaches a level at which it can compete in Northwest Europe."
-- Jan Kedzior
Iraq crude exports hold steady in September; UAE emerges as major buyer
India tops China as largest Iraqi oil importer
UAE imports rise to 619,000 b/d in September
Basrah Medium exports reach 1.7 million b/d
Iraq's September crude exports of 2.48 million barrels/day were largely steady month over month, at roughly 75% of prewar levels, according to port loading data obtained exclusively by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
The ongoing crisis in the Strait of Hormuz has created new trade flows, with the UAE emerging as one of the biggest buyers of Iraqi crude in September, importing 619,000 b/d of mostly Basrah Medium. The UAE had, until recently, imported only a rare cargo of Iraqi crude to process at its Ruwais refinery. Since June, UAE imports of Iraqi crude have been rising.
China, typically the largest taker of Iraqi crude, was replaced by India, which imported 700,000 b/d, according to the data. China imported 99,000 b/d, importing both of Iraq's key export grades.
Exports of Basrah Medium in September reached 1.7 million b/d, with Basrah Heavy exports totaling 775,800 b/d. Exports of Basrah Heavy climbed 235,000 b/d from 540,000 b/d in August.
-- Lauren Holtmeier, Faleh Al-Khayat
CRUDE MOC: Middle East sour crude complex sees two convergences
51 Dubai partials trade during MOC
Dec-loading Oman, Murban crude cargoes declared
Medium sour differentials snap 3-day rise
The Middle East sour crude complex saw two convergences during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Oct. 23, while cash differentials for key sour crude markers edged lower.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed December cash Dubai, Oman and Murban at premiums of $7.99/b to same-month Dubai futures at market close, down 10 cents/b day over day.
During the MOC, 51 December Dubai partials, each comprising 25,000 barrels, were traded. The sellers were Glencore, Unipec, BP, Mitsui and Mercuria, while the buyers were Trafigura, Phillips 66, BP and ExxonMobil.
Unipec declared a cargo of December Oman crude and Glencore declared a cargo of December Murban crude to Trafigura following convergences of 20 partials in Platts Cash Dubai.
